| **[Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31657v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 38th IEEE International Conference on Tools with Artificial Intelligence (ICTAI 2026). 9 pages, 5 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. We release ATTUNE at https://github.com/amazon-science/attune and OpenRecipes, a leaderboard of tuned and verified training recipes, at https://openrecipes.ai.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures. v2: adds NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel benchmark recipes, production deployment results and the OpenRecipes leaderboard</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[Who Is Sycophantic to Whom? Confident LLMs, Human Deference, and the Collapse of Trust Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31579v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research on sycophancy asks whether large language models (LLMs) bend towards what their users want to hear. We ask the reverse question: when an LLM is confident, consistent, and wrong, do users bend towards it? In a within-subjects experiment, 32 participants learned up to five science topics by chatting with five assistants built on the same model (GPT-5-mini). Unknown to participants, each assistant's briefing had 0, 25, 50, 75, or 100% of its facts replaced by plausible falsehoods, and every assistant spoke with full confidence. A surprise quiz after each topic offered the correct fact, the planted falsehood, and two distractors, with a confidence rating for every answer. Across 104 quizzes and 1,040 answers, scores fell from 7.4 to 2.9 out of 10, and the planted option was chosen on 60% of falsified items against 11% of correctly taught ones (odds ratio 12.5). Transcript coding showed that whenever the assistant stated a falsehood, it was adopted about 65% of the time at every falsification level. Absorbed falsehoods were held with high confidence (8.4/10), the modeled confidence advantage of right over wrong answers vanished, and the discrimination of confidence for correctness fell from an AUROC of 0.70 to chance, while post-task trust ratings barely moved. Participants tended to return to the chat more when the assistant was worse, but returning went with more absorption, not less, and 30% of all turns pasted quiz questions back to the source being checked. The assistant itself was almost never sycophantic and never conceded under pushback. We introduce a baseline-corrected Human Deference Index to quantify this human-side sycophancy and argue that trustworthiness is a property of the human-AI pair, that sycophancy evaluations should measure both directions, and that learning interfaces must make reliability legible claim by claim.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages; 32 figures; 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Social-HUMINT: An LLM-Augmented Unified Framework for Measuring Social Media Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31573v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In the post-API landscape, social-media profiling has become inexpensive and automated: Large Language Models (LLMs) can extract structured information from unstructured content and deduce attributes users never disclosed. A person's true exposure is not what they post but what can be inferred from it. As these practices become routine, exposure to social engineering attacks becomes an operational certainty, demanding a shared framework for assessment and mitigation. We make this exposure measurable by quantifying how accurately a target can be profiled, and how far that profile enables social engineering attacks. Because no existing model fully spans this continuum, we unify Social Media Intelligence (SOCMINT) and Cyber Human Intelligence (Cyber-HUMINT), two individual-focused disciplines that evolved separately, into a single doctrine-grounded taxonomy of 38 capabilities across the Cyber Threat Intelligence lifecycle, where user profiling bridges passive collection and active engagement. Domain experts validated the taxonomy (Fleiss' $κ$ = 0.92). We operationalize its core path (extraction, inference, and susceptibility assessment) as an LLM-augmented multi-agent pipeline, evaluated on real participants' public data. Susceptibility is assessed indirectly: the pipeline predicts how each target would rank personalized engagement options, matching their actual choices significantly above chance. This ranking capacity captures the personalization on which tailored attacks depend. The underlying profiles are accurate: extraction reaches F1 = 97.3%, and inferred attributes score 4.73/5, both far above rule-based and NER-based baselines at negligible cost. The framework augments security analysts' tradecraft rather than replacing it and enables defenders to gauge their own exposure before an adversary does.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantics Delivery Network: Rethinking Web Retrieval Infrastructure for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22486v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly ground their answers in proprietary data and live web content through both single-shot retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and multi-turn agentic search. Yet the web path they use was built for human readers: search services return URLs and snippets ranked by generic relevance, and content delivery networks (CDNs) cache URL-addressed objects without knowing which passage an agent needs. LLMs instead consume short, semantically coherent passages - hereafter, chunks - selected for downstream utility rather than similarity alone, retrieve statefully across reasoning turns, and, as uncoordinated streams, repeatedly fetch, clean, chunk, and embed the same content. We argue that semantic chunk retrieval should become a first-class network-delivery abstraction, and propose the Semantics Delivery Network (SemDN): an origin-authorized, hierarchical substrate that indexes, searches, and caches web content at chunk granularity, serving clients and origins alike. SemDN is more than finer-grained caching: it must estimate missing or stale evidence without an explicit miss signal, let clients choose retrieval representations that origins need not see, and share state among clients with the same representation. Preliminary results show that page-first retrieval processes two to three orders of magnitude more content than agents consume, that agentic queries exhibit task-local chunk reuse invisible to URL caches, and that chunk delivery improves answer quality per context token. We organize an open research agenda around shareable retrieval state, hierarchical caching and refresh under coverage risk, and deployability, including incentives, privacy, and verifiability. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sarendis56/SemDN_HotNets26</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted and to appear at the 25th ACM Workshop on Hot Topics in Networks (HotNets 2026). 13 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking Molecular Text Representations for LLMs: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03057v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for molecular tasks, but it remains unclear which molecular representation to use. We present a systematic benchmark, MolRepBench, to evaluate LLM molecular competence across nine representations and eight chemical tasks. We benchmark 16 LLMs across five model families, including reasoning and non-reasoning variants, chemistry-specialized LLMs, and closed frontier models. Performance is strongly representation-dependent and no single representation wins across tasks, though CML is the best, followed by MolJSON, InChI and canonical SMILES. Explicit structured text representations (CML and MolJSON) dominate structural tasks; IUPAC dominates semantic tasks, winning molecule retrieval for all 16 LLMs; and SMILES variants are rarely optimal despite their prevalence in pretraining. Chemistry-specialized models perform well with SMILES at the cost of large degradations with structured text representations, suggesting SMILES-only evaluation rewards specialization that does not generalize. Using LLM-as-a- judge, we find that IUPAC produces the highest fraction of correct molecule generations. A mechanistic study via tokenization audits, linear probes, and attention shows that representations are encoded differently inside the model; for example, structured representations require more attention across the molecular span. Our results argue against representation-invariant evaluation and motivate task-aware representation routing for LLM-based chemistry.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 10 figures, 21 tables. Accepted at the Main Conference of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[How Well Does Mathematical Reasoning Transfer Across Languages in English-Centric LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02272v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in Reinforcement Post-Training (RPT) have substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), but it remains unclear how well such gains transfer across languages. In this work, we study cross-lingual mathematical reasoning transfer in English-centric LLMs under controlled multilingual evaluation. We first evaluate a range of English-centric reasoning models on multilingual mathematical reasoning benchmarks and find that transfer is highly variable across initial model choice, target language, and training paradigm. Through controlled comparative experiments, we further find that stronger English-centric initialization does not necessarily lead to stronger relative transfer efficiency, even when it yields higher multilingual reasoning accuracy. Finally, we show that the largest improvement comes from moving beyond English-only supervision: adding a single parallel language yields a substantial gain, while further gains from additional languages are smaller but consistent. Overall, our results highlight the need to evaluate multilingual mathematical reasoning directly, using both absolute performance and relative transfer-efficiency metrics.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera ready version for EMNLP 2026 Main</p></details> |
| **[Evaluating the Applicability of LLM Inference Optimizations on Apple Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31479v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Apple Silicon is among the most widely used platforms for local LLM inference, yet nearly all inference optimizations are designed and validated on server-class NVIDIA GPUs under batched serving workloads. Which optimizations retain their benefits when moved to Apple Silicon, and what determines their applicability? We evaluate four optimization families: KV-cache pruning, speculative decoding, ported GPU kernels, and post-training quantization. Experiments span four Apple machines under a common single-request decode workload, with matched RTX 4090 comparisons where possible. KV-cache pruning can reach up to 1.75 times end-to-end throughput on Apple but can reduce throughput on desktop NVIDIA GPUs. Under the common benchmark, all evaluated speculative decoding methods reduce throughput on Apple, despite reported 2-6 times gains on server GPUs. CUDA kernel designs transfer only partially: public Metal lacks asynchronous copy, and batch-one decode makes several serving-oriented mechanisms inapplicable, although transferable components yield up to 1.20 times decode throughput. Quantization gains vary by machine. Across four families, a reported speedup is not a portable property of a technique alone but a joint function of technique, runtime, hardware contract, and workload regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper was accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on On-Device Intelligence (ODI) and the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on SLMs for Agentic Systems (SLM-Agents). OpenReview ODI: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Y6fgNlFNL6, OpemReview SLM: https://openreview.net/forum?id=Hhqi8DIZaG</p></details> |
| **[From Structured Records to Patient Narratives: Preserving Clinical Alignment in LLM-Generated Health Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31467v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As wearable and digital health systems increasingly generate longitudinal physiological records, communicating these observations in patient-accessible language remains a significant challenge. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising mechanism for translating structured clinical information into readable narratives, but this process may introduce semantic drift, where clinically precise meaning is distorted during simplification. We investigate evidence-grounded health summarization for menstrual health monitoring using a constrained generation framework that combines structured evidence conditioning with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) using a prompt-based constrained generation framework. We evaluate semantic alignment using Natural Language Inference (NLI), atomic factuality metrics, and readability measures. Across three LLMs and 4,500 generated summaries, explicit grounding constraints consistently improve clinical alignment, particularly by improving uncertainty preservation and, once faithfulness is evaluated against retrieved evidence, generally preserving or improving faithfulness, while effects on omission remain model-dependent. We also find that improved clinical alignment comes at the cost of increased reading difficulty and medical terminology. These findings highlight the need to jointly evaluate faithfulness, uncertainty preservation, and readability when developing patient-facing medical LLM systems.</p></details> | 18 pages, 3 figures |
| **[When Should LLMs Reason? Adaptive Inference Routing via Entropy Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22873v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve LLM performance, but its benefits vary across tasks, models, and individual queries. On some tasks, CoT provides marginal or negative accuracy gains while substantially increasing token consumption. We investigate whether early decoding dynamics can help identify when explicit reasoning is useful. Our analysis shows that tasks with larger CoT gains tend to exhibit decreasing early-stage entropy, while low-gain tasks more often show oscillatory or increasing trajectories. These patterns motivate an empirical regime interpretation of decoding behavior. We propose \textbf{EDRM} (Entropy Dynamics-based Reasoning Manifold), a lightweight routing framework whose default heuristic requires no router training. EDRM summarizes early entropy trajectories with a compact, interpretable representation and selects among Direct, Standard, and CoT decoding. Across 15 benchmarks and 7 LLMs, its global and instance-level variants offer \textbf{16--55\%} token savings at comparable accuracy. The Full instance-level configuration combines routing with a short Direct fallback branch; our heuristic-router ablation reports up to \textbf{4.9 percentage points} of additional accuracy from oracle answer selection over routing without fallback. These results support selective reasoning based on early decoding signals and demonstrate the potential of entropy-based control for efficient LLM inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[Are Two LLMs Better Than One? A Student-Teacher Dual-Head LLMs Architecture for Pharmaceutical Content Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11957v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for content creation in regulated domains such as pharmaceuticals, where content must be medical accurate and legally compliant. Traditional manual quality control (QC) is slow, error-prone, and create publication bottlenecks. To address this, we introduce a modular, LLM/VLMs-driven QC architecture, LRBTC (Language, Regulatory, Brand, Technical, Content Structure), implemented through a Student Teacher HITL architecture and Waterfall rule filtering logic for scalable, verifiable content validation. This architecture is designed to provide verifiable, traceable, and scalable content optimization. Our methods achieve 83.0% F1 and 97.5% recall on AIReg Bench, reducing missed violations five fold compared with Gemini 2.5 Pro. On CSpelling, it improves mean accuracy by +26.7 %. Our error analysis further reveals that while current models are strong at detecting misspellings (92.5% recall), they fail to identify complex grammatical (25.0% recall) and punctuation (41.7% recall) errors, highlighting a key area for future work. This work provides a practical, plug-and-play solution for reliable, transparent quality control of content in high-stakes, compliance-critical industries. We also provide demo access.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to the Industry Track of Conference; currently under peer review</p></details> |
| **[Major Revision, Minor Accountability: LLMs as Reviewers of Published Articles in Top-Tier Journals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31414v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the growth of scientific output, the peer review system is approaching a breaking point: reviewers are increasingly hard to secure, and turnaround times lengthen accordingly. LLM-assisted or LLM-generated peer review has been proposed as a remedy, but how such models would treat manuscripts already published in top-tier journals, the closest thing the current system has to a gold standard of scientific validity, is not known. Here we assembled a corpus of 200 articles, each released under a CC BY 4.0 license and published in 2026, from 20 general-science, life-science, and clinical-medicine journals including Nature, Science, and Cell, and asked four current LLMs (two proprietary, two open-weight) to produce independent peer-review reports of each one under a blinded protocol. Their decisions were overwhelmingly harsh and starkly inconsistent with each other: 605 of 800 reviews (75.6%) recommended major revision or rejection, but the rate at which any single model did so ranged from 17.5% to 100% (p < 0.001), disagreement on manuscripts that had, by construction, already survived real editorial review at the journals in question. When the same manuscripts were doctored with one deliberately planted, field-specific methodological or statistical flaw, detection rates varied from 17.0% to 60.5% across models (p < 0.001). Recommending revision for papers that had already passed editorial review (low specificity) while missing flaws deliberately placed in front of them (low sensitivity), these models produce output that should be read for what it is: an uneven, framing- and model-dependent signal.</p></details> |  |
| **[Modeling of Therapist-Client Dynamics in Psychotherapy Using LLM-Based Assessments: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12450v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study aimed to develop and validate a computational framework for modeling therapist--client interaction processes and to examine how therapist behaviors and relational qualities relate to client self-disclosure and emotional expression in psychotherapy sessions. We developed automated assessment pipelines using large language models (LLMs) to measure therapist behaviors (empathy, exploration), relational quality (rapport), and client outcomes (self-disclosure, self-directed negative emotion, outward-directed negative emotion). Model-generated scores were validated against human annotations. We then applied these measures to approximately 2,000 hours of psychotherapy transcripts from the Alexander Street corpus. This yielded an estimation sample of 119,233 client utterances from 1,573 sessions and 37 clients. Automated measures showed good to excellent agreement with human annotations (ICC = 0.60 - 0.81), with rapport (ICC = 0.81), self-disclosure (ICC = 0.78), empathy components (ICC = 0.60 - 0.75), and emotion measurements (ICC = 0.66 - 0.80). SEM analyses revealed that therapist empathy and exploration were each associated with increased client disclosure and increased expression of self-directed negative emotions. | **[One Algorithm, Two Goals: Dual Scoring for Parameter and Data Selection in LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06166v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In Large Language Model (LLM) fine-tuning, parameter and data selection are common strategies for reducing fine-tuning cost, yet they are typically driven by separate scoring mechanisms. We cast parameter and data selection as two bilevel selection problems under a common validation objective and derive a shared local response-surrogate scoring rule. Under first- and second-order validation-improvement approximations, parameter importance and data utility emerge as column-wise and row-wise aggregations of a single gradient interaction matrix, yielding a closed-form row-column correspondence for co-extracting both signals. Building on this structure, we propose DualSFT (Dual-Selection Fine-Tuning), a one-shot dual-scoring algorithm that produces a parameter mask and data subset from shared gradient statistics. On 3B-9B LLMs, single-axis DualSFT variants strengthen target-task performance and stability-plasticity trade-offs within their comparison groups, while full DualSFT yields a more favorable joint-constrained trade-off than sequential hybrid baselines under matched budgets.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Complementary Vulnerabilities: Auditing and Governing Clinician-LLM Error Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25279v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Health systems are deploying large language models (LLMs) into clinical workflows, often on the assumption that a second automated check can only reduce error. The two reasoners' failure substrates differ: they are embodied and cognitive in the clinician, and statistical and generative in the model - so their weak points need not line up. That divergence is plausible rather than guaranteed, and the independence it creates is fragile, eroding when clinicians defer to the model or the model conforms to the clinician. We therefore propose a predeployment audit in which each site measures error overlap on its own cases. We also propose a governance model with which the site keeps those errors independent over time. Health systems should then judge a clinical artificial intelligence (AI) tool by whether its failures overlap with their clinicians' failures, as well as by its accuracy.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages main text, 2 figures. Accepted at SMASH 2026, the Symposium on Model Accountability, Sustainability and Healthcare (Mila, Montreal, November 3-4, 2026), after peer review on OpenReview</p></details> |

| **[Behind Aggregate Scores: What Systematically Varying Arena-Hard's LLM Judge Reveals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30512v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM judges are used to determine which of two answers is better. | **[FIGFLEX: A Mixed-Precision FP-INT GEMM Accelerator Co-Designed with Low-Bit LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30480v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Weight-only quantization keeps LLM activations in floating point and stores weights as low-bit integers, turning the dominant workload into floating-point-integer (FP-INT) general matrix multiplication. | **[Environment Duels: A Microcosm of LLM Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30116v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Through a tangle of real-world interactions, from synthetic training pipelines to data shared on the open web, frontier LLMs absorb what their predecessors and competitors create. LLMs erode this barrier by generating functional websites from natural-language prompts, yet prior work does not answer how far LLM-driven generation actually reaches: functionality is assessed by inspection rather than execution; visual fidelity is scored by automated metrics or a few internal coders rather than the humans such pages aim to deceive; evaluation is confined to commercial models; and no study benchmarks generated pages against phishing kits deployed in the wild. PhishGen constructs prompts across three input modalities in seven combinations with twelve safeguard-bypassing rewriting strategies, generates pages using 14 LLMs spanning commercial, open-source, and uncensored categories, and assesses each via automated similarity metrics, reference-based detectors, a crowdsourced user study, and execution-based functionality testing, benchmarking all results against 64 real-world phishing kits. Although 96.2% of safeguard-bypassing outputs render, their perceived fidelity is modest (2.02/5), and only 38.7% complete the full credential-harvesting pipeline, with the Unsaved error as the largest failure mode. Contrary to the belief that uncensored models pose the greatest threat, GPT 4.1 reaches 86.3 to 94.1% functional success while uncensored models rarely exceed 45.4%. Phishing kits remain more complete per sample, yet three strong LLMs surpass the kit-level rate (70.9 to 89.7%), showing that LLMs trade average completeness for scalable, low-barrier generation of working phishing pages. We further identify generator-level fingerprints that current similarity-based defenses miss, and distill recommendations for hybrid detectors.</p></details> |  |
| **[Circuits of LLM Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30076v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. However, their predictions are neither deterministic nor uniformly reliable across samples. How can we combine predictions from different models to improve reliability? We view LLM classifiers as noisy measurement instruments of an underlying target label. Our goal is to maximize average classification accuracy at a given average query cost. To this end, we introduce circuits that organize LLM classifiers into an adaptive sequence of queries and decisions. We then characterize the optimal circuit, obtain the fundamental limits of achievable accuracy, and provide practical insights on realizing the optimal circuit. Across seven classification tasks spanning safety, health, political science, human preference, agent evaluation, decision-making and reasoning, our method improves the cost-accuracy frontier over routing and cascading baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Hitchhiker's Guide to Program Analysis, Part III: Mostly Harmless LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15122v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Filtering out a static-analysis warning asserts that no feasible execution can trigger the reported error, whereas confirming a bug requires only one that does. However, LLM-generated explanations alone cannot establish that the reported error is impossible, and mistakenly filtering out a warning conceals a real bug. Sound static analysis can provide this guarantee, but scalable analyses leave behind the very warnings we seek to filter, whereas sufficiently precise whole-program analysis is impractical. Fortunately, analyzing a small part of the program is often enough to show that a warning is a false alarm. We therefore assign LLMs a different role: rather than judging the validity of warnings, they identify relevant code and construct a harness that supplies the context needed for precise, targeted static analysis. We present Evident, which combines LLMs with the static analyzer Frama-C/Eva. Because a generated harness may exclude inputs or paths that trigger real bugs, Evident restricts how the harness initializes inputs and validates it using the value flow reported by the original detector. A warning is filtered out only when validation passes and Eva rules out the reported error in the harness; otherwise, the warning is kept. On 200 real Android kernel-driver warnings, including 40 confirmed bugs, Evident filters out 48.8-63.8% of the 160 false alarms across four LLMs without missing any confirmed bug, whereas baselines that let LLMs make the final decision miss confirmed bugs with every evaluated model. With GPT-5.5, although these baselines achieve 2.0-5.5 percentage points higher overall accuracy by filtering out more false alarms, they miss 27.5-45.0% of the confirmed bugs. Evident also rediscovers a confirmed vulnerability that had previously been overlooked by both LLM-based filtering and manual review.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Text to Voice: A Reproducible and Verifiable Framework for Evaluating Tool Calling LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Voice agents increasingly require reliable tool use from speech, whereas prominent tool-calling benchmarks remain text-based. A targeted analysis of failure cases demonstrates that degradations most often reflect misunderstandings of argument values in the speech. Considering real-world deployment scenarios, we further report text-only results, an ambiguity-based reformulation stress test, and a reference-free LLM-as-judge protocol validated against human preferences. Notably, we find that open-source Qwen3 judges with at least 8B parameters exceed 80% agreement with proprietary judges, supporting privacy-preserving evaluation. | **[An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. | **[EPIC: Rethinking Trust Regions for Autoregressive LLM Policies via Exact-KL Prefix Importance-Ratio Clipping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29999v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) based on PPO-style clipping has become an effective approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Starting from the monotonic-improvement principle of trust-region policy optimization, we then construct a prefix-level trust region using the exact reverse-KL coordinate $r-1-\log r$, whose behavior-policy expectation equals the corresponding prefix-distribution KL divergence. We also introduce the HiddenLens Corpus (HLC), comprising 312 requirements annotated with 847 hidden assumptions across six NFR categories. HiddenLens outperforms keyword-based and QuARS-Adapted baselines by 71% and 61%, respectively, achieving an end-to-end F1-score of 0.79; its BERT-based NFR classifier achieves an F1-score of 0.847. | **[FaultGene: LLM-Assisted Fault-Augmented Test Generation for Hardware-in-the-Loop Validation of Autonomous Driving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29969v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable validation of autonomous driving cyber-physical systems requires testing not only nominal driving behavior but also failures arising from faults in sensing and embedded computation. | **[Commit or Abstain? | **[Privacy in Context Is Being Overlooked in the Age of General-Purpose LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29925v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) continue to grow in scope and capability, privacy concerns are on the rise. Furthermore, we argue that even future work in this direction needs to wrestle with fundamental tensions across various desirable properties of prompt privacy. | **[Asking a Multimodal LLM Whether Someone Fell: Zero-Shot Screening of Low-Resolution Thermal Video in a Laboratory and an Occupied Home](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29913v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thermal cameras can watch over older people living alone without capturing faces, and multimodal large language models (LLMs) can judge a short video without task-specific training. | **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. | **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. As a result, AI accelerators achieve varying levels of efficiency across the two phases. GPUs are well suited to prefill, but are often underutilized during decode. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. Motivated by this observation, we explore a heterogeneous architecture that integrates GPUs and NPUs, mapping prefill to GPUs and decode to NPUs to achieve high performance and efficiency. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. To address this, we propose HAWK, a transfer mechanism that enables rapid KV cache transfer across heterogeneous devices. HAWK establishes a remote direct memory access (RDMA) path for direct cross-device transfers while handling inter-device tensor layout discrepancies as part of the transfer process. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. | **[UlaCache: Unified Logical Addressing for Sparse LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29820v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long contexts increase the memory cost of the key-value (KV) cache in large language model (LLM) inference. | **[Neither Stochastic Parroting nor AGI: LLMs Solve Tasks through Context-Directed Extrapolation from Training Data Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23323v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This position paper argues that many behaviours framed as either "stochastic parroting" or "advanced reasoning" are better explained as context-directed extrapolation from training-data priors, and that ML and NLP research communities would benefit from evaluations that distinguish remembering and basic extrapolation from genuine application-level generalisation. | **[Extending FunctionGemma for Practical On-Device Mobile Function Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25373v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-device assistants require function-calling models that map natural language to local system actions, but existing resources emphasize web APIs or narrow mobile-action catalogs. We fine-tune the 270M model with TRL supervised fine-tuning under completion-only loss, producing an extended specialist and a combined model trained jointly with Google's MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE. On MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED, end-to-end accuracy improves from 29.3% for the base model and 17.2% for Google's Mobile-Actions variant to 76.5%. The combined model retains 76.5% on MOBILEACTIONSEXTENDED and reaches 82.3% on MOBILEACTIONSGOOGLE, down from the 90.3% of Google's Mobile-Actions specialist, representing an 8.0-percentage-point trade-off in return for doubling category coverage. Includes main paper, references, and appendices. Code, datasets, models, and demo available at GitHub and Hugging Face</p></details> |

A template-parser factorial experiment shows strong complementarity: neither component replacement alone restores parsing from the baseline, whereas their joint replacement recovers 196/200 first-request parses. Across five Qwen2.5-Coder checkpoints, classifier-positive emissions increase with model size while the mismatched interface reports no calls; a separate matched-interface Qwen3 ladder exhibits few silent emissions through 8B. In a preregistered, single-seed, 150-step 7B LoRA experiment, changing only the parser registered in verl increases tool executions from 10 to 16,844. Interface repair thus restores tool-mediated experience without a detected performance gain in this setting. After a fault, agents return to the same tool three or more times in a row in up to 22.2% of trials, though strictly identical repeats are rare. The same work declines to extend that to function calling, where a constraint decides which answers exist rather than how one is written. Declining to call anything is the answer it most easily removes, and the one a router can least afford to lose. We therefore run three conditions over one prompt: free generation, generation stopped at the first line, and both applied together. We evaluate open-weight models from 0.6B to 4B on the same items in English and Korean, comparing the languages item by item. The two-condition contrast is negative on abstention accuracy in four of six cells with intervals excluding zero, and positive in none, costing -29.5 points at worst. On the smallest model in Korean the stop costs -20.0 points, the restriction returns +19.5, and together they leave -0.5. What the restriction gives back is readable output, not judgment. Of the 698 abstentions it repairs, 545 had no readable answer at all and 0 were correct decisions the scoring rule rejected. On items that do need a tool the contrast is positive throughout, and abstention is reported first because it is the registered measure. Both preregistered claims about language fail: Korean does not lose more of the abstentions it holds without the constraint, and the removed mass does not explain what does.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 4 figures, 21 tables</p></details> |

This process creates a closed loop that iterates until coverage converges. For evaluation, we move beyond aggregate accuracy. An LLM judge anchored to gold annotations classifies failures as missed calls, spurious calls, or parameter errors, producing a detailed error profile for each model. We evaluate five model families, including proprietary models and models with open weights, analyze their performance across difficulty levels and error categories, and validate the judge through agreement with human annotations.</p></details> |  |
| **[LogicIF: Towards Complex Logic Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09125v4)** | 2026-08-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Instruction following has catalyzed the recent era of Large Language Models (LLMs) and is the foundational skill underpinning more advanced capabilities such as reasoning and agentic behaviors. As tasks grow more challenging, the logic structures embedded in natural language instructions becomes increasingly intricate. However, how well LLMs perform on such logic-intensive instructions remains under-explored. We propose Logic Instruction Following (LogicIF), a task requiring models to precisely follow and simulate every logic step within complex instructions. To generate these instructions, we construct LogicIFGen, a scalable, automated framework for generating verifiable instructions from code functions, which can naturally express rich logic such as conditions, loops, nesting, and function calls. Using this framework, we curate a collection of complex code functions to construct LogicIFEval, a benchmark comprising 426 verifiable, human-examined, logic-rich instructions. Our experiments demonstrate that current state-of-the-art LLMs still struggle to correctly follow the instructions in LogicIFEval. Most LLMs can only follow fewer than 60% of the instructions, revealing significant deficiencies in their capacity to handle instructions that involve complex logic structures. To further demonstrate the efficacy of our framework, we collect an extensive set of code functions across a wide range of logic difficulties to generate LogicIFTrain, a fully synthesized training set featuring 27,324 verifiable instructions. Our results show that a model trained on LogicIFTrain using reinforcement learning significantly outperforms the base model on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks. All the data, codes and models are released at https://github.com/mianzhang/LogicIF.</p></details> | COLM 2026 Acceptance |
| **[An Agentic AI Framework Overcomes Fundamental Limitations of Large Language Models for Glaucoma Detection from Fundus Photography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07651v1)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) show promise in medical image interpretation but suffer from hallucination, limited accuracy, and run-to-run inconsistency. We developed and validated an agentic AI framework integrating LLMs with specialized deep learning tools for glaucoma detection from fundus photography. The workflow had three steps: (1) LLM initial assessment; (2) function calling to invoke specialized tools for image quality (QAModel, FundaQ-8), glaucoma classification (SwinV2-Tiny), and optic disc/cup segmentation (SegFormer-B0); and (3) LLM reflection integrating the initial impression with tool outputs. Two LLMs (Gemini 2.5 Flash, GPT-5.4 mini) were evaluated on two public datasets (ORIGA, n=100; RIM-ONE-v3, n=100) under uncropped and cropped fields of view; all images were independently graded by a masked fellowship-trained glaucoma specialist. The agentic workflow improved classification accuracy by 16 to 47 percentage points across all conditions, reaching within 6 points of the specialist; on RIM-ONE-v3 the best configurations matched the specialist accuracy of 88%. LLM-alone approaches failed in two ways: GPT-5.4 mini showed positive bias (sensitivity 95-100%, specificity 0-5%), while Gemini 2.5 Flash varied stochastically between runs; the agentic workflow corrected both. Cup-to-disc ratio error fell 15-50% (MAE 0.156-0.228 to 0.104-0.132), and correlation with specialist grading rose from weak (r=0.12-0.39) to moderate-strong (r=0.59-0.84). Run-to-run consistency rose from near-random (kappa as low as -0.01) to near-perfect (kappa up to 0.96). Integrating LLMs with specialized tools addressed key limitations of LLM-alone approaches, including over-diagnosis and run-to-run variability. Gains held for both LLMs, suggesting generalizability across backbones, and may signal a shift from monolithic models toward orchestrated multi-agent systems in medical AI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent Memory Distillation: Empowering Small LLM Agents with Hierarchical Teacher Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07169v1)** | 2026-08-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory systems have shown promise for improving agent performance, but their potential remains largely unexplored for small language models, which struggle to generate sufficient successful trajectories on their own. We propose Agent Memory Distillation (AMD), a training-free framework that transfers structured knowledge from a large teacher agent to a small student agent through hierarchical memory. AMD constructs three complementary memory types from successful teacher trajectories: Workflow memory encodes task-level strategies, Subtask memory provides concrete behavioral examples at an intermediate granularity, and Function memory captures per-function calling conventions and common pitfalls. Workflow and Subtask memories are injected proactively at the start of each task, while Function memory is retrieved reactively upon tool-calling errors. We evaluate AMD on three tool-use benchmarks using four student models (4B-8B parameters) with GPT-5-mini as the teacher, achieving average accuracy gains of 27.2%p, 11.2%p, and 3.4%p on AppWorld, BFCL V3, and ToolSandbox, while consistently outperforming existing memory-based baselines. Further analysis shows that Subtask memory contributes the largest gains, teacher effectiveness depends on both teacher capability and student compatibility, and 4B-sized students benefit most from AMD.</p></details> | Under review |

## LLM Tabular
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Instruction Tuning of Large Language Models for Tabular Data Generation-in One Day](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23220v1)** | 2025-11-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tabular instruction tuning has emerged as a promising research direction for improving LLMs understanding of tabular data. However, the majority of existing works only consider question-answering and reasoning tasks over tabular data, leaving tabular data generation largely unnoticed. In this work, for the first time, we explore the efficacy of instruction tuning in improving LLMs tabular data generation capabilities. More specifically, given the high data and computation requirements of tabular instruction tuning, we aim to address the possibility of instruction tuning for tabular data generation with limited data and computational resources. To achieve this, we first create a high-quality instruction dataset for tabular data, enabling efficient LLM comprehension. We then instruction-tune an open-source LLM (Llama3.1-8B-Instruct) on the training set of this dataset to improve its tabular data generation performance. Our experimental results show that by using our high-quality dataset and instruction-tuning on only 7K instructions with an A100 GPU, for less than 6 hours, we achieve tabular data generation performance on par with the most capable commercial LLM, GPT-4o.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2025), 1st Workshop on Foundation Models for Structured Data</p></details> |
| **[Small Models are LLM Knowledge Triggers on Medical Tabular Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.01570v3)** | 2025-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent development in large language models (LLMs) has demonstrated impressive domain proficiency on unstructured textual or multi-modal tasks. However, despite with intrinsic world knowledge, their application on structured tabular data prediction still lags behind, primarily due to the numerical insensitivity and modality discrepancy that brings a gap between LLM reasoning and statistical tabular learning. Unlike textual or vision data (e.g., electronic clinical notes or medical imaging data), tabular data is often presented in heterogeneous numerical values (e.g., CBC reports). This ubiquitous data format requires intensive expert annotation, and its numerical nature limits LLMs' capability to effectively transfer untapped domain expertise. In this paper, we propose SERSAL, a general self-prompting method by synergy learning with small models to enhance LLM tabular prediction in an unsupervised manner. Specifically, SERSAL utilizes the LLM's prior outcomes as original soft noisy annotations, which are dynamically leveraged to teach a better small student model. Reversely, the outcomes from the trained small model are used to teach the LLM to further refine its real capability. This process can be repeatedly applied to gradually distill refined knowledge for continuous progress. Comprehensive experiments on widely used medical domain tabular datasets show that, without access to gold labels, applying SERSAL to OpenAI GPT reasoning process attains substantial improvement compared to linguistic prompting methods, which serves as an orthogonal direction for tabular LLM, and increasing prompting bonus is observed as more powerful LLMs appear.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ICLR 2025. Codes will be available at https://github.com/jyansir/sersal</p></details> |
| **[Elephants Never Forget: Memorization and Learning of Tabular Data in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06209v3)** | 2024-12-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While many have shown how Large Language Models (LLMs) can be applied to a diverse set of tasks, the critical issues of data contamination and memorization are often glossed over. In this work, we address this concern for tabular data. Specifically, we introduce a variety of different techniques to assess whether a language model has seen a tabular dataset during training. This investigation reveals that LLMs have memorized many popular tabular datasets verbatim. We then compare the few-shot learning performance of LLMs on datasets that were seen during training to the performance on datasets released after training. We find that LLMs perform better on datasets seen during training, indicating that memorization leads to overfitting. At the same time, LLMs show non-trivial performance on novel datasets and are surprisingly robust to data transformations. We then investigate the in-context statistical learning abilities of LLMs. While LLMs are significantly better than random at solving statistical classification problems, the sample efficiency of few-shot learning lags behind traditional statistical learning algorithms, especially as the dimension of the problem increases. This suggests that much of the observed few-shot performance on novel real-world datasets is due to the LLM's world knowledge. Overall, our results highlight the importance of testing whether an LLM has seen an evaluation dataset during pre-training. We release the https://github.com/interpretml/LLM-Tabular-Memorization-Checker Python package to test LLMs for memorization of tabular datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>COLM ...</summary><p>COLM camera ready, fix typo</p></details> |
| **[LLM Embeddings Improve Test-time Adaptation to Tabular $Y|X$-Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.07395v1)** | 2024-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For tabular datasets, the change in the relationship between the label and covariates ($Y|X$-shifts) is common due to missing variables (a.k.a. confounders). Since it is impossible to generalize to a completely new and unknown domain, we study models that are easy to adapt to the target domain even with few labeled examples. We focus on building more informative representations of tabular data that can mitigate $Y|X$-shifts, and propose to leverage the prior world knowledge in LLMs by serializing (write down) the tabular data to encode it. We find LLM embeddings alone provide inconsistent improvements in robustness, but models trained on them can be well adapted/finetuned to the target domain even using 32 labeled observations. Our finding is based on a comprehensive and systematic study consisting of 7650 source-target pairs and benchmark against 261,000 model configurations trained by 22 algorithms. Our observation holds when ablating the size of accessible target data and different adaptation strategies. The code is available at https://github.com/namkoong-lab/LLM-Tabular-Shifts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Elephants Never Forget: Testing Language Models for Memorization of Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.06644v1)** | 2024-03-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While many have shown how Large Language Models (LLMs) can be applied to a diverse set of tasks, the critical issues of data contamination and memorization are often glossed over. In this work, we address this concern for tabular data. Starting with simple qualitative tests for whether an LLM knows the names and values of features, we introduce a variety of different techniques to assess the degrees of contamination, including statistical tests for conditional distribution modeling and four tests that identify memorization. Our investigation reveals that LLMs are pre-trained on many popular tabular datasets. This exposure can lead to invalid performance evaluation on downstream tasks because the LLMs have, in effect, been fit to the test set. Interestingly, we also identify a regime where the language model reproduces important statistics of the data, but fails to reproduce the dataset verbatim. On these datasets, although seen during training, good performance on downstream tasks might not be due to overfitting. Our findings underscore the need for ensuring data integrity in machine learning tasks with LLMs. To facilitate future research, we release an open-source tool that can perform various tests for memorization \url{https://github.com/interpretml/LLM-Tabular-Memorization-Checker}.</p></details> | <details><summary>Table...</summary><p>Table Representation Learning Workshop at NeurIPS 2023</p></details> |
